# Preface / To the highly-praised
<note>Likely referring to the "Highly-praised Brotherhood of the Rose Cross."</note>

...were, he would have easily recognized everything sufficiently; and what had been in him **in potentia** <note>original Latin: "in potentia"; meaning "potentially" or as a latent power.</note> or as a capability, and had all rested sleeping, could have been awakened in him through the observation of other things or the testimony of another's intellect. Because, however, such acuity <note>original German: "scharffsinnigkeit."</note> has been lost, he must either through much slow searching and effort find and attain something—even the cause of a single thing—or grasp and hold onto what another has discovered, or what one alone possesses in full, at the very least through **Faith**.

<margin>The principles of reason. <note>original Latin: "Principia rationis."</note></margin>

<margin>Sense or experience. <note>original Latin: "Sensus sive experientia."</note></margin>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of how the human mind processes sensory information. Having previously described the "pentagogic center" or common sense, the author now explains how the mind organizes individual sensory images into categorized knowledge.</meta>

# Brotherhood of the Rose Cross

<margin>Observation <note>original Latin: "Observatio."</note></margin>
...observes and preserves it. If another such image comes before it another time, it preserves that one in the same way, and does so as often and as much until it seems to it that its **little chamber** <note>original German: "Kämmerlein"; the author uses the metaphor of a small room or cabinet to describe the physical seat of memory in the brain.</note> might be cluttered, and the things lie among one another in disorder, in excess, and in an untidy heap. <margin>Induction and Logical Conclusion <note>original Latin: "Inductio" and Greek: "σύλλογισμός" (syllogismos).</note></margin> Therefore, it then takes and examines one image against the other: if it finds that it is entirely equal to the other—and from its center gives lines of equal length and equal width or surfaces—it then concludes that it is one [kind of] thing. It creates a common **rule and yardstick** for it, so that all other things—which are equal to the divided yardstick in one or another size and division—can be measured, regulated, and judged as to whether they, as many **individuals** <note>original Latin: "individua"; refers to single, distinct objects or beings.</note>, belong to a single **unity** and oneness, or if they are excluded from the same, and how closely they **approach** <note>original Latin/German: "appropinquiren vnd nahen."</note> the **generic center** <note>original Latin: "centro generico." In this context, it refers to the essential core or archetype that defines a specific category or genus.</note>.

And thus out of a **multitude of individuals** <note>original Latin: "ex multitudine individuorum."</note> it makes one **unity** <note>original Latin: "unitet."</note>, which one calls **spe-**

<summary>This page describes the cognitive process of induction, explaining how the mind compares multiple sensory images to form general categories and rules. ...species <note>original Latin: "speciem"</note> and **specific idea** <note>original Latin: "ideam specificam"</note>.

And since this natural ability—which arises from a certain **arrangement** <note>original Latin: "disposition"</note> of the **instruments** <note>original Latin: "Instrumenten"; here referring to the physical senses and organs of the brain.</note>, without which the human soul can perform no work as long as it is united with the body—is a good thing (which the Germans call having a "good head" and the Latins call ***ingenium*** <note>original Latin: "ingenium"; refers to a person's innate talent, wit, or natural mental capacity.</note>), he proceeds. He holds these **specific ideas** <note>original Latin: "ideas specificas"</note> against one another, setting aside those that coincide with each other, and thus creates a new **unity** <note>original Latin: "unitet"</note> and **generic idea** <note>original Latin: "ideam genericam"</note> or **subordinate species** <note>original Latin: "specificam subalternatam"</note>.

And when he has many of these, he climbs higher still, and out of these same **ideas** <note>original Latin: "ideis"</note> and **subordinate species** <note>original Latin: "specibus subordinatis"</note>, he creates a **unity** and **genus** <note>original Latin: "genus"; a general category or class in logic.</note>, until he reaches the **most general genus** <note>original Latin: "genus generalissimum"</note> and the highest **unity**, the **one of all unities** (as **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary figure associated with ancient wisdom and the "Hermetic" tradition central to Rosicrucian thought.</note> says) **which is the root** <note>original Latin: "unam unitatum omnium quæ sunt radicem"</note>. Toward this goal, all things—including the human understanding—silently aim; but very few give off a **reflection** <note>original Latin: "reflexion"</note> of these sunbeams, such that they know they aim there, and know the true end of their knowledge and understanding...

<summary>This page describes the cognitive process of moving from specific observations to general categories, eventually reaching a "highest unity" or "root" of all things. The author frames this as a natural ability of the "ingenium" or human wit, influenced by the physical state of the body.</summary>
<keywords>categorization, ingenium, Hermes Trismegistus, epistemology, species and genus, logic, unity of knowledge</keywords>

...a single unity <note>original Latin: "solum." Completing the phrase from the previous page: "the one of all unities... a single one."</note>. However, it happens all too often that, in such a progression of **reason** and the operations of the **senses**, errors slip in. For **on account of the unsuitability of the instruments and the inequality of the medium** <note>original Latin: "propter ineptitudinem instrumentorum & inæqualitatē medij"</note>—or the unevenness of the means through which **objects** <note>original Latin: "objecta"</note> and things encountered move the senses and act as their appropriate stimuli before they even reach each instrument <note>The "instruments" refer to the physical sense organs like the eyes or ears.</note> of the senses—a blue mist often comes before one’s eyes, so that one mistakes a **cobweb** for a **crocodile**. 

And when such a thing happens frequently, or when many people err with their senses at once and imagine something false, then **reason** also draws a false conclusion and creates a crooked rule. And the higher it then imagines itself to be climbing, the further it wanders from the truth, so that the saying taught in the schools from **Aristotle** <note>The ancient Greek philosopher whose systems of logic were the foundation of university education in the 17th century.</note> rings true:

Likewise, that **the whole is greater than its part**, that two is more than one, etc. Yet beyond this, this infallible rule is certain: **Except for divine revelation and the reflective intellect: Nothing is in the intellect that was not first in the senses.** <note>original Latin: "Excepta revelatione divinâ & intellectu reflexo: Nihil est in intellectu quod non prius fuerit in sensu." This is a cornerstone of empirical philosophy, suggesting that humans can only know what they have experienced through their five senses, unless God reveals it directly.</note>

# Preface / To the Most Praiseworthy

...lovely fellowship <note>German: *Begehunge*. This implies a communal gathering or a way of living together.</note> of your highly honored Brotherhood, and what a gracious love of neighbor will be among you, among whom no hatred, envy, or other such unfaithful behaviors <note>German: *unvertrewligkeiten*. Refers to acts of bad faith or social discord.</note> run through; but rather everything is shared for the worthy. Which mouth will be able to speak the sweetness of such gracious love, which has been planted in you by God, and returns again to God from whom it came and proceeded? By quoting Sixtus of Siena, the text establishes a clear distinction between forbidden sorcery and the "true" mystical study (Cabala) that the author intends to explore.</meta>

# Sixtus of Siena, In the Holy Library
## Book 2, under
->Letter: E.<-

Because all books pertaining to the Cabala <note>original: "CABALAM." Also spelled Kabbalah, this refers to the Jewish mystical tradition. In the late Renaissance, many Christian scholars sought to study it, leading to significant controversy and censorship by the Church.</note> were recently condemned by a decree of the Holy Roman Inquisition, it must be understood that there are two types of **CABALA**: one true, the other false. 

The true and pious kind is that which explains the hidden mysteries of the Holy Law according to anagogy <note>original: "anagogen." Anagogy is the highest of the four traditional levels of biblical interpretation, focusing on the spiritual, mystical, or "heavenly" meaning of the text.</note>. This form, I say, the Church has <gloss>not</gloss> condemned. 

The false and impious **CABALA** is a certain counterfeit type of Jewish tradition which some wicked people claim descended from Moses through the fathers down to themselves. It is full of numbers, vanities, and falsehoods, and is little or no different from Necromancy <note>original: "Nicromantia." This refers to "black magic" or the summoning of the dead, which was a capital crime in many jurisdictions and a grave sin in the eyes of the Church.</note>. This type of superstition, therefore, which is improperly called Cabala, the Church has deservedly condemned in recent years.

<gloss>Sixtus of Siena, Holy Library, Cabala, Roman Inquisition, Jews, Necromancy</gloss>

It serves as a protective preface, justifying the manuscript's subject matter by aligning "true" Cabala with orthodox Christian interpretation.</summary>

A 2

### Iamblichus: On the Mysteries of the Egy-
->ptians.<-

All people wish to be happy, but they feel that the life they desire above all else is not sufficient unless a greater cause of happiness is added. Therefore, it is ridiculous not to labor with every effort, zeal, and industry so that we might attain wisdom <note>original Latin: "sapientiam." In this context, wisdom refers to a divine or spiritual understanding that transcends mere book-learning.</note>, which alone can make us happy. For you will find that those things which are highly esteemed by men are but shadow-sketching <note>original Greek: "σκιογραφίαν" (skiographian). A term used by ancient philosophers to describe a painting that looks real from a distance but is revealed as a crude imitation upon closer inspection; a deceptive illusion.</note>, empty images, and a shaded painting; so that it may be rightly said: Man is nothing, and human affairs are unstable.

The inner man.
Augustine testifies that Adam possessed that more secret wisdom, in *On Genesis against the Manicheans*, book 2, chapter 11. <note>original Latin: "Quod Adam habuit secretiorem illam sapientiam testatur Augustinus, de genesi con. Manich. lib. 2. cap. 11." From God’s Word we know that when **GOD the LORD** initially created man, He gave him not only the external, visible, and tangible body, but also the inner, invisible, and intangible soul, which in Holy Scripture is sometimes called the **inner man** <note>This term, frequently used by the Apostle Paul (e.g., Romans 7:22), refers to the spiritual and intellectual self that is capable of communion with the divine.</note>, who was created according to God’s image and therefore endowed with **Divine Wisdom**. Although this wisdom was considerably obscured and darkened in the first man through the Fall, Adam nevertheless retained all kinds of knowledge and **sciences** <note>original Latin: *scientias*. In the 16th and 17th centuries, "sciences" referred broadly to systematic branches of knowledge or learned disciplines, including philosophy and theology.</note> in his memory even after the Fall—especially the way by which one can again (as far and as much as is granted and permitted to man by God after the Fall) come to the attainment of such Wisdom of God.

<margin>Three kinds of Magic.</margin>
In order to recognize such a distinction, one must know that there are namely three kinds of *Magic*:

<margin>Divine Magic.</margin>
The first and only true *Magic* is the **Divine**, which may otherwise be called *celestial Magic* <note>original: *Magia caelestis*</note> or *divine wisdom* <note>original: *divina sapientia*</note>—that is, Heavenly or Divine Wisdom—and is called by the Hebrews *Mercana* <note>likely referring to Merkavah mysticism or "secret" things</note>, in Latin called the *wisdom of divinity* <note>original: *sapientia divinitatis*</note>, and otherwise named *Kabbalah* <note>original: *Cabala*</note> by them.

<margin>Human Magic</margin>
The second, which indeed may have originated from the first in the beginning, is **Human** *Magic* (among which the *Platonic doctrine* <note>original: *Doctrina Platonica*</note> is also to be counted). This was later so mixed with human laws, ceremonies, and all manner of abuses, and thereby so darkened, that it rightly lost its former name and is for that reason called **Human** *Magic*. This subsequently divided itself further into many *species* or types, of which *Peucer* <note>Caspar Peucer, a 16th-century scholar and physician</note> mentions many in his *book on the types of divination* <note>original: *in libro de generibus divinationum*</note>.

<margin>Superstitious Magic.</margin>

->Preface.<-

-trast, because they themselves despise the word of God and consider **Divine Wisdom** to be **folly** <note>original: *Thorheit*</note>, I am happy and willing to let myself be mocked, regarded as a fool, and scolded by them here with this humble **treatise** <note>original: *Tractat*</note> of mine. This, as I know for certain, will be no shame for me before God and all truly wise people, but rather an honor and glory. I also have no doubt that these **worldly-wise men** <note>original: *Weltweisen*; those learned in secular or pagan philosophy</note> will try to prevent this highly useful and healing work with their utmost and best diligence. This is because they worry that it might give their **philosophy** and worldly wisdom a great shock. They will step against it, indeed with hands and feet <note>A German idiom meaning to resist with all one's might; similar to the English "tooth and nail."</note>, to ensure such things are not allowed to emerge, and they will try to block and resist it. They would not do all this, however, if they truly looked within themselves and considered the saying of their own worldly-wise scholar, the pagan philosopher **Seneca**, which states: *"O, what a contemptible thing is man, unless he raises himself above human things"* <note>original Latin: *O quam contemta res est homo, nisi ultra humana se erexerit*</note>.

Since this treatise, therefore,

## Concerning the Highest and Noblest Good

Saint Augustine writes that the primary <term>endeavor</term> <note>original Latin: *studium*</note> of all <term>philosophers</term> <note>original Latin: *Philosophorum*</note> was this: namely, how one might live happily and well in the world. Concerning this, all manner of strife and <term>disputations</term> <note>original: *disputat*</note> arose among the pagan philosophers, so that one held this and another held that to be the same "highest good," and defended it in his writings. ### Concerning the Highest and Noblest Good.

[...sear]ched; we Christians should all the more diligently investigate and eagerly seek after the same <term>Highest Good</term> <note>original: *höchsten Gut*; often referred to by scholars as the *summum bonum*, or the ultimate purpose of human existence</note>, especially since such a desire for it has been inherited and inborn in humanity since the time of <term>Adam</term> <note>In the biblical tradition, the first human from whom all others descend</note>. This is easily observed from the fact that the crafty old serpent—the wretched Devil—could find no better way to reach the first humans (even while they were in their state of perfection) or to compel them to act against God’s earnest command and eat the fruit of the tree standing in the middle of Paradise, than by firmly persuading them that they would thereby gain the knowledge to distinguish good from evil. That is to say, they would then know what the most noble, precious, and best Good truly was. This desire to know such things was so great in our first ancestors, even as perfect human beings, that they allowed themselves to be captivated and persuaded by the Devil (though he spoke falsely and with lies) to disregard and set aside the commandment of God their Creator and to fall away from Him. The laity and common people, however, have held as the highest and best good that thing toward which each person was most inclined <note>original: *affectionirt*; a term derived from Latin meaning to have a strong emotional or mental leaning or "affection" toward an object or goal</note>; each person has also striven with all diligence to attain that same thing: Thus, those who held riches, money, and property to be the highest Good have also directed their entire purpose, livelihood, and trade toward how they might make themselves very wealthy (whether it happens by right or by wrong). Those, however—whether they be learned people or common layfolk—who held prestige <note>original: *ansehen*; referring to social standing, reputation, or honor</note> to be the <term>Highest Good</term> <note>original Latin: *Summum bonum*; the ultimate goal of human life</note>, have also struggled and pushed with all possible diligence toward how they might, through noble—

### Chapter 3.
## Of Worldly Wisdom.

What was cited above regarding Wisdom might perhaps be understood by most people as referring to **worldly wisdom**, because even the cleverest people in this world know of almost no other thing than their own wisdom. However, that such worldly wisdom cannot be the **highest good** <note>original: *Summum bonum*</note> can easily be deduced and measured by anyone who considers the uncertainty, doubtfulness, and imperfection of human **philosophy** <note>original: *Philosophia*</note>. For one only needs to look first at the **Seven Liberal Arts** <term>Sieben freye Künste: the standard medieval and Renaissance university curriculum, comprising the Trivium (grammar, logic, rhetoric) and the Quadrivium (arithmetic, geometry, music, astronomy)</term> within it: Is it not the case with most of them that no true, actual, stable, and harmonious certainty is to be found? Because of this, those who study them and explain them through debate and disputation fall into such discord with one another that—to say nothing of the **dialecticians**, **rhetoricians**, **astronomers**, and **geometricians** <note>originals: *Dialecticorum, Rhetoricorum, Astronomorum, Geometrarum*; practitioners of logic, public speaking, and the mathematical sciences</note> (whose **controversies** and **disputes** <note>originals: *concertationes* and *disceptationes*</note> are indeed visible in public print every day)—even the **grammarians** <note>original: *Grammattici*</note> [engage] against one another in violent and

<summary>This page argues that worldly wisdom and human philosophy cannot be the "highest good" because they are defined by uncertainty and constant scholarly infighting. The author points to the internal contradictions and public disputes among practitioners of the Seven Liberal Arts as evidence of this imperfection.</summary>

### Chapter 4;
### That the wisdom of this world is held as foolishness before God.

The ancient teacher Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636 AD), a highly influential scholar and archbishop in the early Middle Ages.</note> writes that whoever is wise according to the world is certainly to be regarded as nothing other than a fool before God. By this, he strikes down human wisdom to such a degree that it is rightly no longer to be esteemed, let alone that it should be considered the highest and best good.

Regarding such foolishness and folly of worldly wisdom, we also have clear testimonies in the Old and New Testaments.

<margin>Isaiah 29:14. 1 Corinthians 1:19.</margin>

<header>The Worldly-Wise Oftentimes</header>
<meta>catchword: little treatise</meta>

In this place we should and could—in confirmation of the aforementioned title—cite all kinds of specific testimonies from the books and writings of the worldly-wise philosophers, showing how they often behave so *shamefully* <note>original Latin: *turpiter*</note> in their wisdom, *confuse* <note>original: *confundiren*</note> themselves, wander astray, and become so entangled that it certainly appears more a foolishness than a true wisdom. However, because such a task would become too much or too long in this place, and since their writings are already full of such foolishness, doubt, and *contradictions* <note>original: *Contrarieteten*</note>, we wish hereby to have *referred* <note>original: *referiret*</note> and directed the reader to those very sources. 

But for anyone for whom the *perusing* <note>original: *percuriren*, meaning to run through or read over</note> of so many various books and writings might perhaps be burdensome, let him just take up and read without trouble *Cornelius Agrippa’s little treatise* (of which mention was made above in the third chapter) which he wrote *on the vanity and uncertainty of all sciences* <note>original Latin title: *de vanitate & incertitudine omnium scientiarum*</note>; there he will find sufficient *absurdities*, *stupidities*, and *follies* <note>original Latin: *absurdidates*, *stoliditates* & *ineptias*</note> cited therein.

## Chapter 6.
### That Worldly Wisdom is Fleeting and Has No Permanence.

Thus, from the previous chapter, we have heard that all <term>worldly wisdom</term><note>The author uses *Weltweißheit* to describe secular philosophy and human science as opposed to divine revelation.</note> has been found to contain little truth or certainty, little perfection, and indeed little that is truly upright, wise, or clever. Instead, it must pass away along with those who cling to it, as Paul shows. In short, it is all the same: <margin>Ecclesiastes, last chapter</margin> *Vanity of vanities and all is vanity* <note>original Latin: *Vanitas vanitatum & omnia vanitas*; a famous line from Ecclesiastes 1:2 and 12:8 emphasizing the temporary nature of life.</note>. ### Worldly Wisdom

many years now, I have diligently studied the three main <term>faculties</term><note>The traditional university subjects: Theology, Law, and Medicine.</note> one after another. In each of these, I have industriously searched through all kinds of writers, and have otherwise read many and various books in the **humanities**<note>original: *humanioribus*; the "Studia Humanitatis" or liberal arts including grammar, rhetoric, and history.</note>. Yet, in the end, I have become quite tired and weary of them, because I found little in them that was thorough, certain, or lasting.

Especially, however, I found it quite burdensome and vexing that most who have written books in one **faculty** or another, or in any **science**<note>original: *scientia*; used here in the broad sense of any systematic branch of knowledge.</note>, were not inclined toward—nor did they have the intention of—how they might **investigate**<note>original: *indagiren*; from the Latin *indagare*, meaning to track or hunt for something.</note> the right and actual truth. This is despite what **Cicero** said:
<margin>Cicero, On Duties, Book 1.</margin>
"For it is characteristic of man to seek and investigate the truth."<note>original: *Quod hominis si propria Veri inquisitio atque indigatio*</note>
Instead, they were more concerned with how they might **debate** a matter **for and against**<note>original: *pro & contra*</note> or **on both sides**<note>original: *in utramque partem*; a classical rhetorical exercise of arguing both for and against a position.</note>. Or perhaps they sought to defend their preconceived opinions against their **adversaries** with all sorts of subtle **arguments**, wishing thereby to make a name and a reputation for themselves. Thus, their foremost **intent** is directed only toward envy, ambition, quarreling, and debating. This they do
<margin>Basil, Preface to Proverbs.</margin>
regardless of the fact that their own **axiom**<note>original: *Axioma*; a self-evident truth or fundamental principle.</note> states:
<margin>Publilius Syrus.</margin>
"Truth is lost through excessive quarreling."<note>original: *Nimium altercando veritas amittitur*; a famous maxim by the Roman writer Publilius Syrus, often attributed to "Publius the Mime."</note>

# Chapter 14.

## On whether there are many others who are gifted with the Wisdom of God.

Although very many people have lived since the beginning of the world, very few have ever been found among them who attained the Wisdom of God, as can be clearly understood from the Holy Scripture; for because among all people, so very few—indeed, rarely one—is to be found who lets such a high gift of God and the highest good be a matter of truly zealous earnestness to him, it is also not at all surprising why in this mortal life so very few persons are deemed worthy by God the Lord that...

## On whether more [are gifted] with Wisdom

...they become capable of and partakers in such Divine Wisdom and the highest good.

<margin>Sirach 6:25</margin>
Therefore the wise man Sirach <note>Also known as Ecclesiasticus, a book of wisdom in the Apocrypha</note> says in the Latin text: *Wisdom, according to its name, is a teaching that is not revealed to many.* <note>original: "Sapientia, N. doctrina secundum Nomen est ejus, & non est multis manifesta."</note> That is:

This teaching of Divine Wisdom is not known or revealed to many of them.

Likewise, although the Lord Christ, while He walked on earth, had a great number of people—reaching several thousand listeners—to whom He preached by interpreting the Word of God that which was necessary for them to know for their salvation and blessedness: He nevertheless did not publicly explain to them the way to the Wisdom of God, nor how and in what manner it may be attained in this world; instead, He presented this almost always through **parables** <note>original: "parabolas"; allegorical stories used to convey spiritual truths</note> or allegories.
<margin>N.B.</margin>
<margin>Mark 4:33–34</margin>
To His disciples alone, however, He interpreted such parables when the other listeners were absent. Therefore He said to them: **“To you it is given to understand the mystery of the kingdom of heaven, but to these it is not given.”**
<margin>Matthew 13:11</margin>

For since to all the <term>Worldly-wise (Weltweisen)|In this context, scholars, philosophers, or those who rely solely on human reason and secular logic.</term>, the Wisdom of God is a folly <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 1:23, where Paul notes that the Gospel is "foolishness to the Greeks."</note> (as shall be explained further below), they also seek in vain for such a thing which is <note>original Latin: "contrarium"</note> contrary and opposed to their own; and therefore, according to their own rule, it cannot coexist with the same in one <term>Subject (Subjecto)|In 17th-century philosophy, the "subject" is the underlying vessel or person in which a quality (like wisdom) resides. # To Attain Wisdom #

...is **deprived of wisdom.** <note>original: *sapientia defraudatus est*. This completes the quote from Sirach 37 begun on the previous page, regarding how the "sophist" or intellectual pretender loses access to truth.</note> By this, he intends to give us to understand that whoever seeks to intermingle their <term>Sophistry|The use of clever but false arguments, especially for deceiving others or showing off intellectual prowess.</term> makes themselves hateful to God and will never arrive at the true Wisdom of God. For as long as one does this, they cannot have the grace from God necessary to attain His Wisdom. Therefore, one must renounce worldly wisdom and truly find within themselves that which was explained above in Chapter 4: namely, that the wisdom of this world is foolishness in the eyes of God.

<margin>3.</margin>
Thirdly, one must truly and properly recognize the **foolishness** in which they are mired; for such is the very best preparation and entrance to true Wisdom. Therefore the wise man **Sirach** says: 

<margin>Sirach 51:27</margin>
> I lifted my hands toward heaven, and then my soul was enlightened through Wisdom, so that I recognized my foolishness. 

The **Apostle Paul** looked toward this same truth when he said: 

<margin>1 Corinthians 3:18</margin>
> If any man among you seems to be wise in this age, let him become a fool, so that he may be wise. 

Regarding this, **Lactantius** <note>Lucius Caecilius Firmianus Lactantius (c. 250–325 AD), an early Christian author who attempted to bridge the gap between Greek philosophy and Christian theology.</note> also wrote rightly and well: 

<margin>Lactantius, Book 6, Chapter 23</margin>
**The first step of wisdom** <note>original: *Primus gradus sapientiæ*. The full thought in Lactantius usually concludes that the first step of wisdom is to know what is false, and the second is to know what is true.</note>

# Concerning the Path and Process

<margin>
Marsilio Ficino, *On the Rapture of Paul*, Book 7. <note>original: "Marsilius Ficinus de Repudial. 7." This likely refers to Ficino's "De raptu Pauli," a work on mystical ascent, though the OCR spelling "Repudial" is likely a transcription error for "Raptu." Ficino was a 15th-century priest and philosopher who led the revival of Plato in the Renaissance.</note>
</margin>

With divine matters, it is so established that they cannot be taught like human arts and wisdoms. Instead, if the soul is properly purified and converted, it receives an understanding of divine things, as the highly experienced and learned man Marsilio Ficino has written. Therefore, this same art of heavenly wisdom has been called by some in the Hebrew language "Cabala" <note>Kabbalah, a Jewish mystical tradition involving the reception of secret knowledge.</note> or in Latin *Receptio* <note>original: "Receptio," meaning "the act of receiving."</note>, which in English <note>original: "Deudsch" (German)</note> means as much as an art that one attains through revelation from God.

<margin>
Reuchlin, *On the Miraculous Word*, book 1, chapter 5; and Galatino, *On the Secrets of Catholic Truth*, book 1, chapter 6. <note>Johannes Reuchlin (1455–1522) and Pietro Galatino (1460–1540) were famous Christian scholars who studied Hebrew mysticism to better understand Christian theology.</note>
</margin>

However, the knowledge that one person can obtain from another in this regard consists primarily in the path and process by which to arrive at such high art of divine wisdom. Specifically, it teaches how a person should <term>prepare|the Latin term "praepariren" implies a ritual or spiritual setting of oneself in order</term> himself, and how he should purify and convert his soul so that it is fit and ready to receive such a supreme gift of God.

<margin>
Wisdom 1:4
</margin>

For wisdom does not enter into a malicious soul, and does not dwell in a body subjected to sin.

<margin>Sirach 32:20</margin>

The wise man **Sirach also** calls this preparing the heart and sanctifying the soul. Likewise, it is to accommodate the mind. And elsewhere, Scripture calls it **buying such gold as is refined with fire**, and putting on wise garments so that the nakedness of one's shame is not revealed. Likewise, it is an eye-salve with which to anoint the eyes so that one may see. <note>These are allusions to the Book of Revelation, where spiritual wealth and vision are contrasted with physical poverty and blindness.</note>

<margin>Bernard, *Sermons on the Song of Songs*, sermon 47.</margin>
The Church Fathers called this preparation the <term>active life|vitam activam: A stage of spiritual development focused on moral discipline, labor, and the purging of vices.</term>. However, they called the exercise of Divine Wisdom itself the <term>contemplative life|vitam contemplativam: A higher stage of spiritual life focused on the direct, prayerful gaze upon God and heavenly truths.</term>. Therefore Bernard has written that the **active life** <note>original: "vita activa"</note>, or this same preparation, is the way **toward the contemplative life** <note>original: "ad vitam contemplativam"</note>, which

-># On the Path and Process<-

<margin>Gregory, Book 16 of the *Moralia* <note>Gregory the Great (c. 540–604), a Pope and Doctor of the Church known for his influential commentary on the Book of Job.</note></margin>
is toward Divine Wisdom. For as Gregory intends, the glory of God must be sought through His paths. On the other hand, it would be an entirely backward order

<margin>Bernard, *Sermons on the Song of Songs*, sermon 45.</margin>
if one were to seek the **contemplative life before the active life** <note>original: "Vitam contemplativam ante Activam"</note>; that is, to speak plainly: if one were to sit and wait like the <term>Enthusiasts|Enthusiasten: A term used in the Reformation era to describe those who believed they received direct "inner" revelations from God without the need for scripture or outward effort.</term> and do nothing toward it until a roasted chicken flies into one's mouth. <note>A traditional German idiom referring to someone expecting rewards without doing any work.</note>

Therefore, one who wishes to obtain the wisdom of **God** must also do their part (as far as preparation and readiness are concerned) in the matter, as Sirach says:

<margin>Sirach 6:28 <note>In modern Bibles, this citation is often Sirach 6:19-22.</note></margin>
> One must endure effort and labor for a short time for the sake of wisdom, but very soon one will enjoy her fruits.

And again he says:

<margin>Sirach 51:35 <note>In modern Bibles, this is Sirach 51:27.</note></margin>
> Look at me: I have had effort and labor for a short time, and have found great comfort.

Such labor and preparation, which must be employed and used beforehand to obtain the wisdom of God, is commonly called in Holy Scripture by the single word <term>discipline|disciplina</term>, which is translated as **upbringing** or **discipline** <note>original: "Zucht"</note>. # Of Divine Wisdom.

<center>K</center>

These are now no common matters that one can learn thoroughly and by necessity from general textbooks or the preaching of most theologians; rather, they are great and highly weighty things that must be learned solely from the Wisdom of God, and from the aforementioned Spirit of Grace and Prayer.

<margin>Augustine on Psalm 19. There, the first knowledge of God is to know what a human being is. <note>original Latin: "Ibi prima Dei cognitio est scire quod homo sit."</note></margin>
**Sixteenthly,** one learns from the Wisdom of God to know the true **inner man**—from where, how, and what he is. Without this knowledge, a person cannot easily come to the knowledge of God. Afterward, one then also learns to rightly and truly recognize God the Lord Himself: how and what He is in His essence and according to His attributes.

<margin>Baruch 3 v. 14.</margin>
Therefore the prophet Baruch diligently exhorts us toward such Wisdom, saying: **"So learn now true Wisdom, so that you may experience who it is that gives long life, goods, joy, and peace."**

# On the Effect and Fruit

## What especially the author of this treatise / through Divine Wisdom / has experienced in the way of exceedingly great mysteries / and what other fruit and effects he has otherwise obtained from it.

At this point, many a person reading this treatise will prick up their ears and can hardly wait until they have heard what I—who write of these things—have received in the way of benefit and fruit from the Wisdom of God. If I could now boast in this place that I had thereby come into much money and goods, or into great worldly honor, pomp, prestige, and glory, then certainly many <term>disciples</term> <note>original: "discipuli". Sixthly, I have discovered therein the true origin from which all and so many diverse languages come; regarding this, I have designed and created an <term>Archetypal Mirror</term> <note>original Latin: *Speculum Archetypum*. Yes, what is more: although otherwise such a great many words seem beyond counting, I can and will indicate from this same mirror the certain, fixed number of each and every word.

In 17th-century music, these were the melodic and harmonic building blocks or "formulas" used to construct a composition.</note>, for which I have also designed and created a foundational mirror. Consequently, even the most practiced and excellent **Composer** will not be able to devise a single musical phrase that I cannot show him in that same mirror; just as I have just said regarding words, I can also name and indicate from it the certain number of so many, and thus all, musical phrases.

Eighthly, it is known to everyone that it is considered no small **miracle of God** that the Lord God allows so many thousands upon thousands of people to be born into the world who are all so different from one another in their faces. <meta>This page continues the argument that worldly philosophers reject spiritual truth because it contradicts their secular training. It follows the analogy from the previous page, where a scholar of worldly logic is compared to a peasant who cannot understand complex philosophy.</meta>

# Divine Wisdom is held by the world...

...soon [thinks]: "Oh, what a completely foolish and unheard-of thing this is!" Likewise: "How is it possible that this and that could be so, or happen thus?" etc. The reason for this is that such a philosopher has not only never heard or experienced anything like it before, but in his worldly wisdom <term>worldly wisdom</term> <note>original: "Weltweißheit." This refers to human reason, logic, and secular philosophy, which the author contrasts with spiritual insight.</note> he has also studied the exact opposite and contrary.

But let a person who—through the grace of God—might otherwise have a desire and longing for Divine Wisdom, not be led astray by this at all. Instead, let him take comfort in the fact that God the **Lord** has not called many who are wise according to the flesh <term>wise according to the flesh</term> <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 1:26, meaning those who are considered intelligent, educated, or elite by human, physical standards rather than spiritual ones.</note>. Rather, He has chosen that which is considered foolish and silly by the world, so that He might put the worldly-wise to shame. 

Therefore, if anyone (among those who feel a desire and inclination toward the wisdom of God within themselves) thinks himself wise, let him gladly become a fool in this world so that he may become truly wise. For this reason, Tertullian <note>An early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155 – c. 220 AD) known for his rigorous and often paradoxical defenses of faith against pagan philosophy.</note> also said: *No one is wise, unless he becomes a fool to the world.* <note>original: "Sapiens nullus, nisi seculo stultus fiat."</note> No one can or may become truly wise, unless...

<summary>This page explains why scholars of worldly philosophy find spiritual truths absurd, noting that their secular education actively contradicts divine logic. It encourages seekers to embrace being seen as "fools" by society in order to attain true spiritual wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>worldly wisdom, divine wisdom, worldly-wise, foolishness, Tertullian, grace of God, 1 Corinthians 1:26, philosopher</keywords>

--- Page 220 ---

## The 21st and Final Chapter.
# Regarding the Difference between Divine and Human Wisdom.

From that which has been set forth above throughout this entire treatise, the reader should now reasonably have sufficient understanding of what notable and great differences exist between the **Wisdom of God** and the **shrewdness of this world**. However, to represent this even more clearly here at the conclusion, I wish to identify even more distinctions by repeating what was mentioned before. As to what great differences exist generally between these two types of wisdom, **God the Lord** testifies through the **Prophet Isaiah**, saying:

### One should not search into the great
### mysteries right at the beginning.

<margin>a Isaiah 28:9.</margin>
The prophet Isaiah **a** wishes that knowledge be taught to those who are weaned from milk and taken from the breasts. That is, those who have either progressed so far in their understanding that they already know how to properly and correctly distinguish and recognize what is black or white, good or evil; or those who are already quite perfect <note>In this context, "perfect" implies spiritual maturity or completion rather than sinless perfection.</note> in this high state. The Apostle Paul had this in mind **b** when he wrote to the Corinthians as follows:
<margin>b 1 Corinthians 3:1 and following.</margin>
> And I, dear brothers, could not speak with you as with spiritual people, but as with fleshly <note>original: "Fleiſchlichen," referring to those still ruled by worldly or carnal desires rather than the Spirit.</note> people, as with young children in Christ. I have given you milk to drink and not solid food. For you were not yet able, nor are you able even now, because you are still fleshly.

### Concerning the most prominent member of this Society, who he may be.

<sig>X iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: united-</meta>

The holy Society should be primarily directed toward this: that where two or more bind themselves together, with and toward one another, for the highest <term>knowledge</term> <note>original: "scientiam"; referring here to a deep, systematic, and experiential understanding of spiritual truths rather than mere academic learning.</note> of Divine Wisdom, they must be mindful in every way—if their Society is to be lasting and useful—that they also bring the **Lord Christ** himself, as the Son of God, into such brotherhood and fellowship of theirs. Which, if they undertake and begin such a Society of theirs rightfully and in the name of **Jesus**, will not be difficult for them to achieve. Since the **Lord Christ** himself has so kindly offered himself for this, where he says: <margin>Matt. 18 v. 20</margin> **Where two or three are gathered in my name, there am I in the midst of them.** Accordingly, such a company or fellowship must not only exist between the people who establish such a holy Society and brotherhood with one another; <margin>1 John 2 v. 3</margin> <note>While the margin cites 1 John 2:3, the following text closely paraphrases 1 John 1:3 regarding fellowship with the Father and Son.</note> but it must also primarily be with the Father and his Son **JESUS CHRIST**. When these are included in the fellowship, then the fellowship and Society is perfect; it is holy; it is highly useful; and ever-constant. Yes, it is a true spiritual betrothal and marriage; indeed, a holy sacrament and true prototype of that union through which one day all the chosen children of **GOD** shall be gathered into a united-

<summary>This page defines the spiritual core of the "holy Society," asserting that true fellowship requires the presence of Jesus Christ and the Father to be perfect and enduring. It frames this community not just as a human organization, but as a mystical "spiritual marriage" and a precursor to the ultimate union of all God's children.</summary>

->Concerning the Most Eminent Fellow Members<-

...steadfast **Body** <note>The text likely completes a word like "standhaftes" or "behartes," describing the enduring nature of the spiritual community.</note> / shall and must be united. Just as it has now been presented one after another, how a human and physical companion in such a holy society should behave toward another, all and each of these things the companions and brothers must diligently observe in themselves—and all the more toward their most eminent fellow-brother and companion, the **Lord Jesus Christ**, and His Father.

<margin>c 1 John 4:12</margin>

Whoever, therefore, holds his faithfulness toward God so constantly / toward them He is faithful in return / Since the Scripture of the Old and New Testaments testifies of Him / that He is faithful and just. Yes, David further says / that He is faithful in all His works. And the wise man <note>Referring to the author of the Wisdom of Solomon, often attributed to Solomon in this period.</note> speaks of this from his own experience thus:

> Those who trust in God / they experience that He keeps [faith] truly / and those who are faithful in love / He does not let them be taken from Him.

Therefore King Hezekiah <note>original: "Hiskia"</note> reminds / the LORD God / that he had walked before Him in truth. Conversely, however, we know / that Ananias was very severely punished / because he lied to God and the Holy Spirit. It is however [expected] of one who wishes to enter into such a holy society...

# Of the Foremost Fellow-Member.
<meta>catchword: golte</meta>

<margin>

<margin>
u Proverbs 20:23.
w Isaiah 57:15.
x Sirach 3:20, 21.
y 1 Peter 5:5, 6.

</margin>
Fifthly, such a person <note>referring to a member of the spiritual society</note> must not only be humble toward his fellow-companion <note>original: "Mitgesellen," a term from craft guilds used here for spiritual peers</note> (as mentioned above) but much more so toward God as the **principal** <note>original: "principaln," meaning the head or director of the society</note> of this holy community. For God will receive those / who are of a humble spirit / and wishes also to dwell with those / who are of such a humble spirit. Therefore the wise Sirach says: The higher you are / the more you should humble yourself / and the Lord will be favorable to you / for the Lord is the highest of all / and does great things through the humble. So also the Apostle Peter and James admonish / saying: Hold fast to humility / for God resists the proud / but gives grace to the humble. So humble yourselves now under the mighty hand of God / that He may exalt you in His time. And to Ezra, the angel said in a vision: Therefore you are pleasing before the Most High / because you humble yourself before Him, as is fitting for you / and do not consider yourself worthy / to be honored among the righteous. In this way, such humility will also be rewarded by God through a reciprocal likeness...

### of this Member of the Society, who he may be.
---

...[hand] was held <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page: "So humble yourselves now under the mighty hand of God," which was considered a sign of humility.</note>; for not to mention the exuberant humility which the Lord Christ <margin>b Philippians 2:7, 8.</margin> practiced, in that he emptied himself <note>Theologians call this *kenosis*, the act of Christ voluntarily setting aside his divine attributes to become human.</note>, took upon himself the form of a servant, humbled himself so far, and became obedient unto the death of the cross. Just consider what an exceedingly great humility this is of Christ, that he offers <margin>c Matthew 18:20</margin>—wherever two or more gather in his name—that he also wishes to be present in such a company and to be in the midst of them.

Finally, as was also required above, that one should willingly and charitably share of his means with his fellow-companion <term>Mitgesellen: A term traditionally used for journeymen or peers within a craft guild; here it refers to fellow Christians.</term>; such is all the more required to be done toward God and his Son. For so says the wise King Solomon: <margin>d Proverbs 3:9.</margin> **d** Honor the Lord with your wealth, and with the firstfruits of all your income. Not that God the Lord needs our worldly goods for himself, but that we <margin>e Psalm 41:1, 8 and following.</margin> should thereby serve our neighbor, as <margin>f Proverbs 14:31.</margin> Solomon himself explains when he says: **e** He who has mercy on the poor honors <margin>g Tobit 4:7 and following.</margin> God. Therefore the elder **g** Tobit <note>A figure from the Book of Tobit in the Apocrypha, known for his piety and almsgiving.</note> gives his son this teaching: Do not turn away from the poor, and so God will look upon you again with grace. Where you can, help the needy; if you have much, give abundantly; if you have little, give that little with a faithful heart, then you will gather a good reward in the time of need. And Sirach **h** says:

# Of the Most Distinguished Companion.

<margin>Ecclesiasticus 14:15 and following</margin>
You must, after all, leave the fruit of your bitter sweat to others, and hand over your labor to your heirs; give gladly, so you shall receive in return, and sanctify your soul, for when you are dead, you have consumed your portion. Likewise:
<margin>Ecclesiasticus 4:10–11</margin>
Behave toward the orphans <note>German: *Weiſen*. In this context, it refers to *Waisen* (orphans) rather than *Weisen* (wise men), following the biblical source in Sirach 4:10.</note> like a father, and toward their mothers like a master of the house; then you will be like a son of the Most High, and He will love you more than your own mother does. And the Apostle Paul <margin>Hebrews 13:16</margin> writes thus: do not forget to do good and to share, for such sacrifices are well-pleasing <margin>Luke 19:6 and following</margin> to God. The Lord Christ also accepted the tax collector Zacchaeus as a disciple all the more because he willingly gave half of his goods to the poor. As He also otherwise commanded His <margin>Luke 12:33 and Mark 10:21</margin> disciples that they should sell what they had and give alms; they should thereby make for themselves purses that do not grow old, and a treasure that never fails, etc. Therefore He attributes all the good that one does for the poor and needy to Himself, <margin>Matthew 25:40</margin> as if it had been done to His own body. Therefore, whoever in this regard sows sparingly <note>German: *Kömlich*, likely a variant of *kärglich* (sparingly), reflecting the first half of 2 Corinthians 9:6.</note> <margin>2 Corinthians 9:6</margin> shall also reap sparingly; and whoever sows in blessing <note>original: *im Segen*, meaning bountifully or generously.</note>. That is, the Lord God will, in this holy <term>Society: a spiritual fellowship or community of believers mentioned throughout the text.</term>, also do His part in return. He will also give them all kinds of bodily blessings <margin>Deuteronomy 28:2 and following</margin> in return, for because the fear of God is the beginning of wisdom. So
